Learning with Importance Weighted Variational Inference: Asymptotics for Gradient Estimators of the VR-IWAE Bound
Kamélia Daudel, François Roueff

Abstract
Several variational bounds involving importance weighting ideas generalize the Evidence Lower BOund (ELBO) for marginal likelihood optimization, such as the Importance-weighted Auto-Encoder (IWAE), Variational Rényi (VR) and VR-IWAE bounds. Yet, it remains unclear how the joint choice of bound and gradient estimator impacts the behavior of the resulting variational inference (VI) algorithms. This paper provides a unified theoretical comparison of reparameterized (REP) and doubly-reparameterized (DREP) gradient estimators tied to the IWAE, VR and VR-IWAE bounds. Through asymptotic analyses of the Signal-to-Noise Ratio as the number of Monter Carlo samples N N goes to infinity, we identify a bias-variance tradeoff in these gradient estimators and we formally justify the superiority of DREP over REP in importance-weighted VI. An additional asymptotic analysis for challenging regimes, where both N N and the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the variational and posterior densities go to infinity, indicates that importance-weighted VI gradient estimators point in a well-founded direction even when the variational approximation deteriorates. Together, these complementary results characterize the optimization trajectory in importance-weighted VI from poor initialization to final convergence. Importantly, our proof techniques establish general theoretical tools for the study of sample means ratios whose scope extend beyond VI and constitute an independent contribution to the field of Monte Carlo methods.

eywords
Variational Inference
\cdot

Alpha-Divergence
\cdot

Importance Weighted Auto-encoder
\cdot

Reparametrization trick
\cdot

1
Introduction

Variational inference (VI) methods seek to find the best approximation to an unknown target posterior density within a more tractable family of probability densities
\mathcal{Q}

(
Neal & Hinton, 1998
;
Jordan et al., 1999
;
Blei et al., 2017
)
. In the standard Bayesian setting, the model is typically assumed fixed and the aim is to identify the member of
\mathcal{Q}
that minimizes a certain measure of dissimilarity to the posterior. More generally, VI is applicable to models depending on a parameter
\theta
, in which case the primary goal is to optimize the marginal log-likelihood. In both scenarios, the intractability of the marginal likelihood necessitates the maximization of variational bounds, which involve the variational family
\mathcal{Q}
and are constructed as surrogate objective functions to the marginal log-likelihood that are amenable to optimization.

While the most traditional variational bound is the Evidence Lower BOund (ELBO), an active line of research in VI is interested in deriving alternative bounds that can improve on the outcome of VI. In particular, popular generalizations of the ELBO relying on importance weighting ideas have been proposed, such as the importance-weighted auto-encoder (IWAE) bound in
Burda et al., 2016
that is parametrized by the number of Monte Carlo samples
N
and the Variational Rényi (VR) bound in
Li & Turner, 2016
that is parametrized by the Rényi alpha-divergence parameter
\alpha
.
Daudel et al., 2023
also introduced the VR-IWAE bound, a variational bound depending on
(N,\alpha)\in\mathbb{N}\times[0,1)
that unifies the ELBO, IWAE and VR bounds. A shared feature of these variational bounds is that they can readily be optimized using reparametrized gradient estimators
(
Kingma & Welling, 2014
;
Rezende et al., 2014
;
Burda et al., 2016
;
Li & Turner, 2016
;
Tucker et al., 2019
;
Daudel et al., 2023
)
.

Yet, the theoretical understanding of how the joint choice of importance-weighted variational bound and gradient estimator governs the optimization trajectory remains limited. The properties of the variational bounds themselves are relatively well-established:
Maddison et al., 2017
;
Nowozin, 2018
;
Domke & Sheldon, 2018
study the asymptotic behavior of the IWAE bound as
N\to\infty
, with
Daudel et al., 2023
exploring the more general VR-IWAE case.
Furthermore, recent works investigate the desirable characteristics of the variational approximation at optimality for the VR bound
(
Margossian et al., 2024
;
Margossian & Saul, 2025
)
and the IWAE bound
(
Cherief-Abdellatif et al., 2025
)
. Despite these insights into importance-weighted variational bounds and their minima, a comprehensive analysis of their gradient estimators is currently missing from the literature.

More precisely, current analyses of reparameterized (REP) and doubly-reparameterized (DREP) gradient estimators, which are the primary gradient estimators in importance-weighted VI, are fragmented and incomplete. Although
Daudel et al., 2023
capture the asymptotics of the REP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound as
N\to\infty
, the role of
\alpha
is completely overlooked outside of the distinction between the pathological case
\alpha=0
(IWAE) identified in
Rainforth et al., 2018
and the case
\alpha\in(0,1)
. As for the DREP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound, existing results are restricted to an informal argument specific to the IWAE setting
(
Tucker et al., 2019
)
.
Beyond these theoretical gaps, there is also a general lack of understanding regarding how REP and DREP gradient estimators perform as the quality of the variational approximation deteriorates.

As a result, comparisons between REP and DREP gradient estimators have been confined to empirical studies suggesting that tuning
\alpha
improves performance and that the DREP gradient estimator outperforms the REP one
(
Li & Turner, 2016
;
Daudel et al., 2023
;
Geffner & Domke, 2020
;
Dhaka et al., 2021
)
. Our work addresses these limitations through two main contributions to importance-weighted VI, both of which are grounded in broader theoretical results for the Monte Carlo estimation of sample mean ratios we establish. Namely, our contributions are as follows:

(i)

First unified comparison between REP and DREP gradient estimators in importance-weighted VI.

We establish an asymptotic characterization of the expectation and variance for Monte Carlo estimators taking the form of a ratio of sample means. By applying this theory to importance-weighted VI, we obtain asymptotic analyses as
N\to\infty
that hold under mild conditions and that allow the systematic comparison of REP and DREP gradient estimators tied to the IWAE, VR and VR-IWAE bounds. Our analyses are based on the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), a metric commonly used in VI that quantifies the quality of an estimator via the ratio of its expected value to its standard deviation. We show that the hyperparameter
\alpha
is responsible for a bias-variance tradeoff in the REP and DREP gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound. Moreover, we provide the first theoretical justification for the empirical superiority of DREP over REP in importance-weighted VI.

(ii)

First study of importance-weighted gradient estimators in the challenging regime where the variational approximation deteriorates.
We analyze the case where the quality of the variational density deteriorates in the gradient estimators. This approach is motivated by observing that the asymptotic regimes in (i) may only kick in for a large
N
in practice if the variational density chosen within
\mathcal{Q}
does not match the posterior density well enough. Specifically, we derive a second analysis at the SNR level when both
N
and the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the variational and posterior densities go to infinity. Our insights indicate that importance-weighted VI gradient estimators point in a well-founded direction even in such settings. As with our first contribution, the technical tools used in (ii) are valid outside of importance-weighted VI, which signals that their utility might extend beyond the context considered here.

Crucially, our theoretical analyses in (i) and (ii) are complementary analyses that reflect different stages in importance-weighted VI algorithms. While (ii) captures the behavior in challenging settings such as poor parameter initialization, (i) corresponds to settings where the variational density is an accurate approximation of the posterior density, such as during training. In addition, provided that the variational family is sufficiently expressive, we obtain that optimization under the challenging regime of (ii) drives the trajectory toward the well-behaved regime of (i). We validate our theory over three experiments of increasing complexity, before outlining directions for future research. Deferred results, proofs and numerical experiments can be found in the appendix.

2
Background

Consider a model with joint distribution
p_{\theta}(x,z)
parameterized by
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{a}
, where
x
denotes an observation and
z
is a latent variable valued in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In Bayesian inference, one seeks to sample from the posterior density
p_{\theta}(z|x)
. However, in many important cases sampling directly from the posterior density is impossible. This then hinders usual inference tasks such as marginal likelihood optimization, where the goal is to find the optimal
\theta\in\Theta
which maximizes the marginal log likelihood

\displaystyle\ell(\theta;x)=\log\left(\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\mathrm{d}z\right).

(1)

To tackle this challenge, VI methods introduce a probability density
q_{\phi}(z|x)
parameterized by
\phi\in\Phi
, whose distribution is easy to sample from and where typically
\Phi\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{b}
. Specifically, in the context of marginal likelihood maximization, VI methods solve an optimization problem involving a variational bound, that is a lower bound on the marginal log likelihood
\ell(\theta;x)
. While the most common example of variational bound is the ELBO

\displaystyle\mathrm{ELBO}(\theta,\phi;x)=\int q_{\phi}(z|x)\log w_{\theta,\phi}(z;x)\penalty\ \mathrm{d}z,\quad w_{\theta,\phi}(z;x)=\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{q_{\phi}(z|x)}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2)

several alternatives to the ELBO based on importance-weighting ideas have been introduced. Two notable instances of importance-weighted variational bounds are the IWAE bound
(
Burda et al., 2016
)
and
the VR-IWAE bound
(
Daudel et al., 2023
)
, where the latter is defined for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and all
\alpha\in[0,1)
by

\displaystyle\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}q_{\phi}(z_{i}|x)\log\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}w_{\theta,\phi}(z_{j};x)^{1-\alpha}\right)\mathrm{d}z_{1:N}.

(3)

The VR-IWAE bound serves as a unifying objective that bridges several existing VI frameworks, inheriting appealing theoretical and practical properties from these connections. It recovers the ELBO when
N=1
(or as
\alpha\to 1
) and the IWAE bound
(
Burda et al., 2016
)
when
\alpha=0
.
Daudel et al., 2023
also show that it is a principled approximation of the VR bound (
N\to\infty
), a flexible variational bound which seeks to improve on the ELBO by employing Rényi’s alpha-divergences
(
Li & Turner, 2016
)
. We expand below on the desirable properties of the VR-IWAE bound originating from its ties to the ELBO, IWAE and VR bounds.

(i)

Tightness.
Increasing the sample size
N
and/or decreasing
\alpha
in the VR-IWAE bound yields a tighter lower bound on the marginal log likelihood provided that the support of
p_{\theta}(\cdot|x)
is contained within the support of
q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
: for all
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1)
with
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ELBO}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N+1}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\ell(\theta;x)

(4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ELBO}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\ell^{(\alpha^{\prime})}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\ell^{(0)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\ell(\theta;x),

(5)

where
\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)
denotes the VR bound from
(
Li & Turner, 2016
)
and is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\left(\int q_{\phi}(z|x)\penalty\ w_{\theta,\phi}(z;x)^{1-\alpha}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}z\right),\quad\alpha\in[0,1)\cup(1,\infty).

(6)

More precisely,
Daudel et al., 2023
, Theorem 3
establishes that: as
N\to\infty

\displaystyle\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)=\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)-\frac{1}{2N}\gamma^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}+o\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)

(7)

\displaystyle\mbox{where}\quad\gamma^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathbb{V}_{Z\sim q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)}\left(\frac{w_{\theta,\phi}(Z;x)^{1-\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)}(w_{\theta,\phi}(Z^{\prime};x)^{1-\alpha})}\right),

(8)

meaning that the VR-IWAE bound is a finite sample approximation of the VR bound which gets tighter and converges to the VR bound at a fast
1/N
rate.

(ii)

Ease of optimization.
The VR-IWAE bound can be straightforwardly optimized via stochastic gradient ascent
(SGA;
Robbins, 1951
;
Bottou et al., 2018
)
paired up with the reparameterization trick
(
Kingma & Welling, 2014
;
Rezende et al., 2014
;
Burda et al., 2016
;
Daudel et al., 2023
)
. The reparameterization trick is a common tool used in VI which has led to empirical advantages when estimating gradients with respect to
\phi
and which relies on the assumption that
Z=f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)\sim q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
with

\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}

(
Kucukelbir et al., 2017
;
Xu et al., 2019
;
Mohamed et al., 2020
)
. Under the assumption that
Z
can be reparameterized, the gradient of the VR-IWAE
bound
\nabla_{\theta,\phi}\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)
reads

\displaystyle\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{q}(\varepsilon_{i})\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{j},\phi;x);x)^{1-\alpha}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\penalty\ w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{\ell},\phi;x);x)^{1-\alpha}}\penalty\ \nabla_{\theta,\phi}\log w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{j},\phi;x);x)\right)\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{1:N}

(9)

and since this gradient can be estimated by the unbiased Monte Carlo estimator

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{m,j},\phi;x);x)^{1-\alpha}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{m,\ell},\phi;x);x)^{1-\alpha}}\penalty\ \nabla_{\theta,\phi}\log w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{m,j},\phi;x);x)

(10)

with
\varepsilon_{m,1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{m,N}
being i.i.d. samples generated from
\tilde{q}
for all
m=1\ldots M
, the resulting procedure enjoys the typical convergence guarantees of unbiased SGA
(
Hoffman et al., 2013
)
. In particular, (
10
) recovers the unbiased gradient estimator used for ELBO optimization in
Kingma & Welling, 2014
when
N=1
(or
\alpha\to 1
) and the one used for IWAE bound optimization in
Burda et al., 2016
when
\alpha=0
. Moreover, (
10
) is used in
Li & Turner, 2016
as an approximation of
\nabla_{\theta,\phi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)
to derive a practical SGA scheme based on the VR bound as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta,\phi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{\int\tilde{q}(\varepsilon)\,w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x);x)^{1-\alpha}\nabla_{\theta,\phi}\log w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x);x)\,d\varepsilon}{\int\tilde{q}(\varepsilon)\,w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x);x)^{1-\alpha}d\varepsilon}.

(11)

Consequently, on top of encompassing the typical ELBO and IWAE optimization frameworks, the VR-IWAE bound provides theoretical grounding for SGA schemes employing (
10
) with
\alpha\in(0,1)
: while these schemes are biased when viewed through the angle of the VR bound, they are unbiased when the objective function is the VR-IWAE bound
(
Daudel et al., 2023
)
.

By unifying the ELBO, IWAE and VR bounds, the VR-IWAE bound allows one to treat the choice of the variational bound as a problem of hyperparameter tuning within a shared framework. This motivates a closer examination of how
(N,\alpha)
influence the optimization trajectory, which we will achieve in this paper via a thorough characterization of key importance-weighted gradient estimators associated to the VR-IWAE bound.

3
Analyzing Importance-weighted VI Gradient Estimators

We begin by defining the notation and the gradient estimators that form the basis of our study.

3.1
Notation and Gradient Estimators Definitions

In the following, we let
x
be a fixed observation,
\alpha\in[0,1)
,
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{a}
and
\Phi\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{b}
be two open subsets, and we assume that for all

\theta\in\Theta,\phi\in\Phi
, the following holds:

(A3)

\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\;\nu(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty
,
\int q_{\phi}(z|x)\;\nu(\mathrm{d}z)=1
and

q_{\phi}(z|x),p_{\theta}(z|x)>0
for
\nu
-a.e.
z
.

Here
\nu
is a
\sigma
-finite measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, typically
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as used
in (
1
)–(
11
), or the Lebesgue measure
on a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or any other convenient dominating measure
on the Borel sets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. To avoid specifying the dominating
measure, we will use the notation
\mathbb{E}
throughout the paper. If the
probability distribution of the involved random variables has not
been specified beforehand, we use a subscript to the symbol
\mathbb{E}
to
indicate their densities with respect to
\nu
. Using this convention,
(
3
) and (
6
) are then respectively
written as

\displaystyle\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{Z_{j}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}w_{\theta,\phi}(Z_{j};x)^{1-\alpha}\right)\right)

(12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim q_{\phi}(z|x)}\left(w_{\theta,\phi}(Z;x)^{1-\alpha}\right),

(13)

with
(A3)
ensuring

w_{\theta,\phi}(\cdot;x)
is well-defined and positive a.s. under the expectation

\mathbb{E}
.

On top of
(A3)
and in order to compute gradient estimators, we assume that the mappings

\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}(x)
,
(z,\theta)\mapsto p_{\theta}(z|x)
,
and
(z,\phi)\mapsto q_{\phi}(z|x)
are differentiable on
\Theta
,

\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Phi
, respectively. We also assume that integration with respect to
q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
can always be reparameterized as an expectation
independent of
\phi
, and that the involved functions of
\theta
and

\phi
inside this expectation are differentiable, meaning
(A4)
below holds:

(A4)

There exist a function
f
and a
density
\tilde{q}
such that
f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)\sim q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)

with
\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}
. In addition, for
\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}
, we
have a.s. that the mapping

\phi\mapsto f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)
is differentiable on
\Phi
.

For a differentiable function
g
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
with

D\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
,
\partial_{y_{k}}g(y)
denotes the partial
derivative of
g(y_{1},\ldots,y_{D})
with respect to
y_{k}
evaluated at

y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
as in (
14
) and

\nabla_{y}g(y)
denotes the gradient vector

\left(\partial_{y_{k}}g(y)\right)_{k=1,\dots,D}
as
in (
9
). When differentiating, it can be
convenient to evaluate the resulting function at a different variable:
we write
\left.\left[h(y,z)\right]\right|_{y=y^{\prime}}
to indicate that the multivariate
function
(y,z)\mapsto h(y,z)
is evaluated at
(y^{\prime},z)
. In the
following, we let
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and

\varepsilon_{m,1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{m,N}
be i.i.d. samples
generated from
\tilde{q}
for all
m=1\ldots M
, we set

Z_{m,j}=f(\varepsilon_{m,j},\phi;x)
for all
j=1\ldots N
and
all
m=1\ldots M
and we let
\psi
denote a component of the

\mathbb{R}^{a+b}
-valued variable

(\theta,\phi)=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{a},\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{b})
. We next define below the two gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound that are central to our study.

Definition 1

(REP and DREP gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound)

The reparameterized (REP) gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound w.r.t.
\psi
is given by

\displaystyle\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{w_{\theta,\phi}(Z_{m,j};x)^{1-\alpha}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\penalty\ w_{\theta,\phi}(Z_{m,\ell};x)^{1-\alpha}}\penalty\ \partial_{\psi}\log w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{m,j},\phi;x);x).

(14)

Futher assuming
\psi
is a component of the

\mathbb{R}^{b}
-valued variable

\phi=(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{b})
, the doubly-reparameterized (DREP) gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound w.r.t.
\psi
is given by

\displaystyle\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(h_{m,j}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\log w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon_{m,j},\phi^{\prime};x);x)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}\right),

(15)

where, for all
m=1\ldots M
and all
j=1\ldots N
,

\displaystyle h_{m,j}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)

\displaystyle=\alpha\penalty\ \frac{w_{\theta,\phi}(Z_{m,j};x)^{1-\alpha}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}w_{\theta,\phi}(Z_{m,\ell};x)^{1-\alpha}}+(1-\alpha)\penalty\ \left(\frac{w_{\theta,\phi}(Z_{m,j};x)^{1-\alpha}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}w_{\theta,\phi}(Z_{m,\ell};x)^{1-\alpha}}\right)^{2}\;.

(16)

The REP and DREP gradient estimators are both unbiased estimators of the gradient of the VR-IWAE bound

(
Daudel et al., 2023
)
. Establishing such properties rigorously requires assumptions to interchange derivatives and expectations, which are typically taken for granted and omitted in VI
see, for instance,
Daudel et al., 2023
. For the sake of completeness, we provide precise sufficient conditions ensuring that the interchange is valid in
Appendix

A
:
(A

df

REP

)
for the REP estimator and
(A

df

DREP

)
for the DREP estimator. We now briefly comment on the specificities of the REP and DREP gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound.

As previously mentioned, the REP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound acts as a bridge between the REP gradient estimator of the ELBO (
N=1
or
\alpha\to 1
), the REP gradient estimator of the IWAE bound (
\alpha=0
) and the (biased) REP gradient estimator of the VR bound (
\alpha\in(0,1)
). The DREP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound (
15
) was introduced in
Daudel et al., 2023
as a generalization of the DREP gradient estimator of the ELBO
(
Roeder et al., 2017
)
and of the IWAE bound
(
Tucker et al., 2019
)
. The main motivation behind DREP gradient estimators comes from the observation made in
Roeder et al., 2017
that:

\displaystyle\partial_{\psi}\log w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x);x)=\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\log w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi^{\prime};x);x)-\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\log q_{\phi^{\prime}}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)|x)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}.

This identity reveals that the REP gradient estimator of the ELBO (
N=1
in (
14
)) has a nonzero variance at optimality, since the left-hand side above has nonzero variance when
q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
matches the exact posterior density
p_{\theta}(\cdot|x)
everywhere due to the score function term
\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\log q_{\phi^{\prime}}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)|x)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}
. By contrast, the DREP gradient estimator of the ELBO (
N=1
in (
15
)) enjoys a variance which goes to zero as the variational density approaches the posterior one, and is shown in
Roeder et al., 2017
to lead to improved empirical performance.

In the spirit of
Roeder et al., 2017
, the DREP gradient estimator of the IWAE bound
(
Tucker et al., 2019
)
and of the VR-IWAE bound
(
Daudel et al., 2023
)
maintain the zero-variance property at optimality. However, the behavior of these gradient estimators during the optimization procedure compared to their REP counterparts is much less straightforward. To clarify these dynamics and avance the field of importance-weighted VI, we seek to provide the first informative asymptotic analyses for the REP and DREP gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound enabling their comparison. To that end, we will start by proving a new general result capturing the asymptotic behavior in expectation and variance of sample mean ratios as the number of Monte Carlo samples increases.

3.2
Key Technical Result on the Asymptotic Moments of Sample Mean Ratios

We present below a key technical result which captures the asymptotic
behavior in expectation and variance for ratios of sample means as the
number of Monte Carlo samples increases.

Theorem 1

Let
(X_{1},W_{1}),(X_{2},W_{2}),\dots
be
i.i.d. with the same distribution as a generic pair
(X,W)
valued in

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Denote, for all
N\geq 1
,
\overline{X}_{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}
and
\overline{W}_{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}W_{i}
. Suppose that there exists
\eta>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{u>0}\left(u^{-\eta}\;\mathbb{P}\left(W\leq u\right)\right)<\infty.

(17)

Then the following assertions hold.

(i)

If
\mathbb{E}(|X|)<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\overline{X}_{N}}{\left(\overline{W}_{N}\right)^{\mu}}\right)=\frac{\mathbb{E}(X)}{\left(\mathbb{E}(W)\right)^{\mu}}\;.

(18)

(ii)

If
\mathbb{E}(|X|)<\infty
,

\mathbb{E}(W^{2})<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}(|X|\,W)<\infty
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}N\,\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\overline{X}_{N}}{\left(\overline{W}_{N}\right)^{\mu}}\right)-\frac{\mathbb{E}(X)}{\left(\mathbb{E}(W)\right)^{\mu}}\right)=\frac{(\mu+1)\mu\,\mathbb{E}(X)\,\mathbb{V}\left(W\right)}{2\,\left(\mathbb{E}(W)\right)^{\mu+2}}-\frac{\mu\,\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}\left(X,W\right)}{\left(\mathbb{E}(W)\right)^{\mu+1}}\;.

(19)

Moreover, if instead

\mathbb{E}(|X|)<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}(W)<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}(\left|\mathbb{E}(W)X-\mathbb{E}(X)W\right|W)<\infty
,
then the convergence in (
19
)
still holds when
\mu=1
with a limit expressed by

\lim_{N\to\infty}N\,\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\overline{X}_{N}}{\overline{W}_{N}}\right)-\frac{\mathbb{E}(X)}{\mathbb{E}(W)}\right)=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(W\left[\mathbb{E}(X)W-\mathbb{E}(W)X\right]\right)}{\left(\mathbb{E}(W)\right)^{3}}.

(20)

(iii)

If
\mathbb{E}(|X|^{2})<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{V}\left(\frac{\overline{X}_{N}}{\left(\overline{W}_{N}\right)^{\mu}}\right)=0\;.

(21)

(iv)

If
\mathbb{E}(|X|^{2})<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}(W^{2})<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N\,\mathbb{V}\left(\frac{\overline{X}_{N}}{\left(\overline{W}_{N}\right)^{\mu}}\right)=\frac{\mathbb{V}\left(\mathbb{E}(W)X-\mu\mathbb{E}(X)W\right)}{\left(\mathbb{E}(W)\right)^{2\mu+2}}\;.

(22)

Moreover, if instead
\mathbb{E}(|X|)<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}(W)<\infty
and

\mathbb{E}(\left|\mathbb{E}(W)X-\mathbb{E}(X)W\right|^{2})<\infty
, then the convergence in
(
22
) still holds when
\mu=1
thus giving

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N\,\mathbb{V}\left(\frac{\overline{X}_{N}}{\overline{W}_{N}}\right)=\frac{\mathbb{V}\left(\mathbb{E}(W)X-\mathbb{E}(X)W\right)}{\left(\mathbb{E}(W)\right)^{4}}\;.

(23)

The proof of
Theorem

1
is deferred to

Appendix

B
. Observe that
(i)
and
(iii)
establish the intuitive limiting behavior for the expectation and variance of
\overline{X}_{N}/(\overline{W}_{N})^{\mu}
, while
(ii)
and
(iv)
capture the behavior of the corresponding first-order terms. Together,
(i)
-
(iv)
will provide a general framework to derive the asymptotics for the REP (
14
) and DREP (
15
) gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound via selected choices of
\overline{X}_{N},\overline{W}_{N}
and
\mu
. Before proceeding to do so in
Section

3.3
, we comment on the assumptions we make in
Theorem

1
and we discuss the implications of this result for VI and more broadly Monte Carlo methods.

Assumptions.

Essentially, the assumptions made in

Theorem

1
ensure that the quantities of
interest appearing in this theorem are well-defined. This is clear
from the specific conditions associated with assertions

(i)
-
(iv)

(e.g., the integrability condition
\mathbb{E}(|X|)<\infty
in

(i)
that guarantees the existence
of
\mathbb{E}(X)
) and we now discuss the condition
(
17
), which is common to all the assertions

(i)
-
(iv)
.

Assuming that there exists
\eta>0
such that
(
17
) holds ensures that the distribution of

W
does not place excessive mass near
0
, since
\mathbb{P}\left(W\leq u\right)=O(u^{\eta})
as
u\downarrow 0
. Hence this
condition slightly strengthens the right-continuity at zero of the
distribution function
u\mapsto\mathbb{P}\left(W\leq u\right)
by imposing a
power decay of arbitrary low positive exponent. Note that without
right-continuity, the
quantities studied in
Theorem

1
would be
ill-defined due to the nonzero probability of a vanishing denominator
in
\overline{X}_{N}/(\overline{W}_{N})^{\mu}
.
As shown in the lemma below, (
17
) admits
equivalent formulations frequently encountered in the related
literature.

Lemma 1

Set
W_{1},\ldots,W_{N}
be i.i.d. with
the same distribution as a generic random variable
W
valued in

\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then the following three assertions are
equivalent.

(i)

There exists
\eta>0
such that (
17
) holds.

(ii)

There exists
\mu>0
and
N\geq 1
such that

\mathbb{E}((\overline{W}_{N})^{-\mu})<\infty
.

(iii)

There exists
\mu>0
such that

\mathbb{E}(W^{-\mu})<\infty
.

Lemma

1
is a direct consequence of the
more precise statement
Proposition

5
, which can
be found in
Section

B.2
alongside its proof. Conditions akin to
(ii)
, although usually stated for a specific value of
\mu
, are typically used when investigating importance-weighted VI bounds or their gradients
see, e.g.,
Maddison et al., 2017
;
Domke & Sheldon, 2018
;
Daudel et al., 2023
. As for
(iii)
, it appears in
Deligiannidis et al., 2025
, which studies the asymptotic bias of self-normalized importance sampling. The assertion
(ii)
can in fact be equivalently stated with an arbitrary
\mu>0
or an arbitrary
N\geq 1
(see
Proposition

5
), which yields
(iii)
as the special case where
N
is arbitrarily fixed to
N=1
.

Related work and broader impact.

Theorem

1
subsumes several results studying importance-weighted estimators. Specifically,
Theorem

1
contributes to the Monte Carlo literature by broadening the applicability of existing importance-weigthing results while operating under strictly less restrictive assumptions.

Indeed, the most closely related existing studies have focused on deriving (
20
) and (
23
), or specific instantiations of these, under stronger assumptions and different proof techniques compared to ours
(
Rainforth et al., 2018
;
Daudel et al., 2023
;
Deligiannidis et al., 2025
)
; see
Table

1
for a quick overview and more details are also available in
Section

B.5
. Furthermore, to the best of our knowledge, existing studies are entirely restricted to setting where
\mu=1
while
Theorem

1
accommodates the general case
\mu>0
. As we shall see, this extension is particularly relevant in the context of VI and will emerge as a theoretical prerequisite for analyzing the DREP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound. This will permit us to bridge the gap left by
Tucker et al., 2019
and
Daudel et al., 2023
, who proposed the DREP gradients for the IWAE and VR-IWAE bounds, respectively, but did not provide asymptotic guarantees.

Ultimately, our framework provides a theoretical foundation for analyzing importance-weighted estimators, both within and well beyond VI. Applications outside of VI include any setting where an importance-weighted estimator is employed to approximate a quantity of interest, e.g., the fine-tuning of Large Language Models
(
Liao et al., 2026
)
.

Table 1:
Closely-related results subsumed by
Theorem

1
under less stringent conditions

Rainforth et al.,2018

(
20
) and (
23
) with stronger moment conditions on
(X,W)

&
Daudel et al.,2023

and problem-specific choices of
(X,W)

Deligiannidis et al.,2025

(
20
) with stronger moment condition on
|\mathbb{E}(W)X-\mathbb{E}(X)W|W

3.3
Asymptotics of gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound as
N\to\infty

Let us now build a unified framework that enables the comparison between the REP (
14
) and DREP (
15
) gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound. The structure of the REP and DREP gradient estimators suggests that the asymptotic behavior in expectation and variance of these estimators can be derived by applying
Theorem

1
for selected choices of
\overline{X}_{N},\overline{W}_{N}
and
\mu
.
Letting
\alpha\in[0,1)
,
\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}
,

\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\ldots
be i.i.d. copies of

\varepsilon
and denoting

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)=w_{\theta,\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi^{\prime};x);x),\quad\theta\in\Theta\,,\;\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi,

(24)

we notably see that setting

X=(1-\alpha)^{-1}\partial_{\psi}\left[\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right]
,

W=\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}
and
\mu=1
in

Theorem

1
permits to capture the
asymptotics of the REP gradient estimator in expectation and variance. Starting for instance with (
18
), we directly get that, under the assumptions of
(i)
in

Theorem

1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\frac{\mathbb{E}(\partial_{\psi}\left[\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right])}{\mathbb{E}(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha})}.

(25)

While (
25
) provides an asymptotic limit, its practical implications are not immediately transparent. The same conclusion holds for results obtained by substituting the above choice for
(X,W,\mu)
into
Theorem

1
. The remainder of this subsection is then devoted to deriving meaningful insights on the behavior of the REP and DREP gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound by leveraging
Theorem

1
as a theoretical foundation.
To that end, we will rely on the following assumption, which corresponds to a rewriting of the condition (
17
) from
Theorem

1
for
W=\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}
that does not depend on
\alpha
.

(A5)

There exists
\delta>0
such that
\sup_{t>0}\left(t^{-\delta}\mathbb{P}(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\leq t)\right)<\infty
.

The theorem below then analyses the behavior in expectation of the REP gradient estimator (
14
) as
N\to\infty
.

Theorem 2

Assume
(A5)
and
(A

df

REP

)
. Then, as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)+o\left(1\right).

(26)

Further assuming that
\mathbb{V}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right)<\infty
yields: as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)-\frac{1}{2N}\partial_{\psi}[\gamma^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}]+o\left(\frac{1}{N}\right).

(27)

The proof of
Theorem

2
is deferred to

Section

C.1.2
. By
Theorem

2
,
\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
thus converges to
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)
at a fast
1/N
rate, where
\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)
is the VR bound (
6
). We now discuss the methodological implications of
Theorem

2
.

Theorem

2
predicts that the REP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound points on average in the direction which maximizes the VR bound when
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)\neq 0
and which minimizes
\gamma^{(\alpha)}(\phi;x)^{2}
when
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)=0
. In both cases, the REP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound targets a well-founded direction that is expected to improve on ELBO maximization.

Indeed, the VR bound with
\alpha\in(0,1)
is known to penalize under-dispersion, driving
q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
to capture regions of posterior probability mass that the standard ELBO typically ignores
(
Li & Turner, 2016
;
Margossian et al., 2024
;
Minka, 2005
;
Bui et al., 2016
;
Margossian & Saul, 2025
)
. A similar conclusion applies when
\alpha=0
, with the particularity that
\mathrm{VR}^{(0)}(\theta,\phi;x)=\ell(\theta;x)
so that the REP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound points on average in the direction which maximizes the marginal log-likelihood for
\psi\in\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{b}\}
and minimizes
\gamma^{(0)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}=\mathbb{V}\left({p_{\theta}(z|x)}/{q_{\phi}(z|x)}\right)
for
\psi\in\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{b}\}

(
Rainforth et al., 2018
)
.

Since
\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=\partial_{\psi}\ell^{(\alpha)}_{N}(\theta,\phi;x)
under
(A

df

REP

)
,
Theorem

2
provides
an asymptotic result for the gradient of the VR-IWAE bound which is coherent with the one established in
Daudel et al., 2023
, Theorem 3
and reviewed in (
7
).
Note also that, assumption-wise,
(A5)
is inherited from
Theorem

1
,
(A

df

REP

)
permits to leverage the reparameterization trick assumption and
\mathbb{V}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right)<\infty
ensures that
\gamma^{(0)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}
is well-defined.

Our next step to further comprehend the behavior of the REP gradient
estimator (
14
) is to capture the behavior of its Signal-to-Noise ratio
(SNR) as
N\to\infty
. Recalling that for a random variable
X
the
SNR is given by

\mathrm{SNR}[X]={|\mathbb{E}(X)|}/{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}(X)}}
, an
unbiased gradient estimator is expected to be accurate if the SNR is
high (so that the target dominates the additive stochastic error) and
noisy otherwise. Since the summands of the
\sum_{m}
summations
in (
14
) are i.i.d., we have

\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{M,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\sqrt{M}\,\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
and we
can interpret the
\sum_{m}
summations as a way to improve the SNR by
the factor
\sqrt{M}
through averaging, while multiplying the
computational cost by
M
. As such, it suffices to consider the
setting
M=1
in the following and to focus on the role of

(N,\alpha)
in
\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
. We then have the
theorem below.

Theorem 3

Assume
(A5)
and
(A

df

REP

)
. Further assume that
\mathbb{V}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right)<\infty
and that
\mathbb{V}\left({\partial_{\psi}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right)}\right)<\infty
. Then, as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\sqrt{N}\,\dfrac{\left|\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)-\frac{1}{2N}\partial_{\psi}[\gamma^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}]+o\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\right|}{\sqrt{\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)}+o\left(1\right)}\;,

(28)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\mathbb{V}\left(\partial_{\psi}\left(\frac{\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha})}\right)\right)\;.

(29)

The proof of
Theorem

3
is deferred to

Section

C.1.3
. Besides
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)
and
\partial_{\psi}[\gamma^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}]
, which we had already identified as key quantities in
Theorem

2
to analyze the REP gradient estimator (
14
), it is now clear that
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
also plays an important role in the success of this estimator. Note that compared to
Theorem

2
,
Theorem

3
further requires
\mathbb{V}\left({\partial_{\psi}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right)}\right)<\infty
so that
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
is well-defined.

Asymptotic results related to (
28
) appear in
Rainforth et al., 2018
and in the proof of
Daudel et al., 2023
, Theorem 1
, albeit under more restrictive assumptions (see
Section

B.5
). For the specific case
\alpha=0
,

Rainforth et al., 2018
establish that the SNR scales as
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{N})
when learning
\theta
and decays at a rate of
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{N})
when learning
\phi
. Furthermore, they identify the quantities
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(0)}(\theta,\phi;x)
,
\partial_{\psi}[\gamma^{(0)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}]
and
\psi\text{-}V^{(0,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
in their SNR result.
Daudel et al., 2023
show that the SNR decay for
\phi
can be mitigated by setting
\alpha\in(0,1)
, but the quantities
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)
,
\partial_{\psi}[\gamma^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}]
and
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
do not appear explicitly in their SNR result nor are they interpreted.
Theorem

3
thus generalizes the approach of
Rainforth et al., 2018
to the entire range
[0,1)
, and operates under strictly weaker assumptions than those required by the two aforementioned previous works. This in turn yields important implications regarding the role of
\alpha
across the entire range
[0,1)
.

More precisely, when
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)\neq 0
(which is a reasonable assumption to make unless
\alpha=0
and
\psi
is among the components of
\phi=(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{b})
or we are at a local optimum for
(\theta,\phi)
), (
28
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\sqrt{N}\,\dfrac{\left|\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)+o\left(1\right)\right|}{\sqrt{\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)}+o\left(1\right)}\;,

which highlights a bias-variance tradeoff at play in the REP gradient estimator of the VR-IWAE bound via the choice of the hyperparameter
\alpha
. Here, the gradient of the VR bound represents the bias-controlling component, while
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
is the variance term. As
\alpha
decreases from
1
to
0
, the VR bound interpolates monotonically from the ELBO (
\alpha\to 1
) toward the marginal log-likelihood (
\alpha=0
) (a monotonicity property recovered, for instance, by taking
N\to\infty
in (
5
)). While this monotonicity property of the VR bound suggests a preference for values of
\alpha
near zero in marginal likelihood optimization, the practical choice of
\alpha
is in fact constrained by the simultaneous evolution of the variance term
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
. Consequently,
Theorem

3
formally justifies how the tuning of
\alpha
has led to improved empirical performance in tasks seeking to optimize the marginal log-likelihood using the REP gradient estimator (
14
)
(see, e.g.,
Li & Turner, 2016
;
Daudel et al., 2023
)
.

Crucially,
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
can be further interpreted in light of
Roeder et al., 2017
;
Tucker et al., 2019
: the intuition that the score function adds variance to REP gradient estimators of the IWAE bound (
\alpha=0
) is now supported theoretically for the general REP gradient estimator (
14
) (
\alpha\in[0,1)
) by looking at the asymptotics of its SNR as
N\to\infty
. The score function adds variance via
\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\log q_{\phi^{\prime}}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)|x)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}
, which can notably be observed by considering the case
p_{\theta}(\cdot|x)=q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
in (
29
) leading to (see (
115
) of
Section

C.1.3
for details):

\displaystyle\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\mathbb{V}\left(\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\log q_{\phi^{\prime}}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)|x)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}\right).

(30)

Although the DREP gradient estimator (
15
) was designed to reduce the variance of the REP one (
14
), a theoretical justification of this property is currently lacking. We therefore next derive the asymptotic SNR of the DREP estimator to formally compare the REP and DREP approaches. Since the DREP gradient estimator (
15
) only impacts the learning of
\phi
, our analysis focuses on its asymptotic behavior w.r.t.
\phi
. This leads us to the theorem below.

Theorem 4

Assume
(A5)
and

(A

df

DREP

)
. Futher assume that

\mathbb{V}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right)<\infty

and
\mathbb{V}\left({[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)^{2(1-\alpha)})]|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}}\right)<\infty
, where
\psi
denotes a component of the
\mathbb{R}^{b}
-valued variable
\phi=(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{b})
.
The following assertions hold.

(i)

If
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\mathbb{V}\left({\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}\right)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}}\right)<\infty
, then, as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\sqrt{N}\penalty\ \frac{\left|\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{VR}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi;x)+o\left(1\right)\right|}{\sqrt{\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)}+o(1)}

(31)

\displaystyle\mbox{with }\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\mathbb{V}\left(\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)^{1-\alpha})}\right)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}\right)\;.

(32)

(ii)

If
\alpha=0
, then, as

N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}\left[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(0,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right]=\sqrt{N}\penalty\ \frac{\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\psi}[\gamma^{(0)}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}]+o\left(1\right)}{\sqrt{\psi\text{-}V^{(0,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)}+o(1)},

(33)

\displaystyle\mbox{with }\psi\text{-}V^{(0,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\mathbb{V}\left(\frac{\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)^{2}\right)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}}{2\,\mathbb{E}(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x))^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\,\mathbb{E}\left(\left.\left[\partial_{\psi^{\prime}}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)^{2}\right)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}\right)}{\mathbb{E}(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x))^{3}}\right).

(34)

The proof of
Theorem

4
is deferred to
Section

C.1.4
. Similarly to
Theorem

3
for the REP gradient estimator (
14
),
Theorem

4
highlights a bias-variance tradeoff in the DREP gradient estimator (
15
) through the choice of the hyperparameter
\alpha\in[0,1)
.

However, a critical distinction arises when comparing the two quantities
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
and
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
. Unlike
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
, which remains non-zero even when the variational approximation matches the true posterior (
p_{\theta}(\cdot|x)=q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
), we get that
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=0
in that case.
Theorems

3
and
4
thus suggest choosing the DREP gradient estimator over the REP one, aligning with the original intuitions of
Tucker et al., 2019
;
Roeder et al., 2017
. From an optimization perspective, the property that
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}
vanishes near the optimum, hence driving the asymptotic variance of the DREP gradient estimator (
15
) to zero, is referred to as the interpolation condition. When this condition holds, the convergence rate of SGA is known to improve significantly
(
Vaswani et al., 2019
)
.

To the best of our knowledge,
Theorem

4
is the first asymptotic result in importance-weighted VI which supports the use of DREP gradient estimators over REP gradient estimators. While the asymptotic
\sqrt{N}
rate for the SNR of the DREP gradient estimator of the IWAE bound (
\alpha=0
) was in fact informally derived in
Tucker et al., 2019
, Section 8.2
, the focus of
Tucker et al., 2019
was on how the
\sqrt{N}
rate of the DREP gradient estimator improved on the
\sqrt{1/N}
rate of the REP gradient estimator when learning
\phi
thanks to a variance reduction phenomenon. In addition, no conditions ensuring that the informal asymptotic analysis written in
Tucker et al., 2019
held were stated.

In contrast, our approach permits us to show under mild conditions
that the SNR rate scales like
\sqrt{N}
for both the REP and DREP
gradient estimators when
\alpha\in[0,1)
(except when
\alpha=0
and
\psi\in\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{b}\}
in the REP gradient
estimator). Importantly, we go beyond the variance reduction
phenomenon that originates from increasing
N
by taking a closer
look at the constants appearing asymptotically in the SNR of the REP
and DREP gradient estimators. We obtain that within a same class of
gradient estimators (REP or DREP), the tuning of
\alpha
leads to
further variance reduction in parallel to increasing
N
, at the
cost of potentially increasing the bias. Furthermore,
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
and
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
are quantities that enable
the comparison between the REP and DREP gradient estimators, with
theoretical evidence suggesting that the latter is expected to
outperform the former. As an aside, observe that all the
results we obtained extend naturally to the case
\alpha=1

corresponding to the ELBO, albeit in a simpler non-asymptotic way
(see
Remarks

4
and
6
in the appendix for more details).

We now present two insightful examples where
Theorems

2
,
3
and
4
apply and in which all the quantities of interest appearing in these results are analytically tractable.

Example 1

Let
\theta,\phi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Set

p_{\theta}(z|x)=\mathcal{N}(z;\theta,\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
and

q_{\phi}(z|x)=\mathcal{N}(z;\phi,\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
, where

\boldsymbol{I}_{d}
is the
d
-dimensional identity matrix. Consider
the reparameterization given by

Z=f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)=\varepsilon+\phi
where

\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
and the goal is
to learn
\phi
. Then, we can apply

Theorems

2
,
3
and
4
and all the terms
appearing in these results are analytically tractable. In
particular, to emphasize the impact
of the latent space dimension
d
, we set

\theta-\phi=\epsilon\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{d}
where

\boldsymbol{u}_{d}
denotes the
d
-dimensional vector whose
coordinates are all equal to
1
and
\epsilon>0
. Then, we have that:
for all
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
k=1\ldots d
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=\epsilon\alpha+\frac{\epsilon(1-\alpha)\mathrm{e}^{{(1-\alpha)^{2}d\epsilon^{2}}}}{N}+o\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\quad\mbox{as $N\to\infty$},

\displaystyle\phi_{k}\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\mathrm{e}^{(1-\alpha)^{2}d\epsilon^{2}}\left(1+(1-\alpha)^{2}\epsilon^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\phi_{k}\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }\alpha\in(0,1),\\
\epsilon^{2}\mathrm{e}^{2d\epsilon^{2}}\;\left(\mathrm{e}^{4d\epsilon^{2}}-4\mathrm{e}^{2d\epsilon^{2}}+4\mathrm{e}^{d\epsilon^{2}}-1\right)&\mbox{ if }\alpha=0.\end{cases}

The derivation details for this example are deferred to
Section

C.2.2
.

Example

1
showcases how
\alpha
enables a bias-variance tradeoff for the REP and DREP gradient estimators of the VR-IWAE bound. For the REP gradient estimator, the variance term
\phi_{k}\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
is reduced by increasing
\alpha
at the cost of increasing the bias in the gradient of the VR bound
\alpha\epsilon
. Viewing this result in terms of SNR,
Theorem

3
yields: as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\sqrt{N}\epsilon\left(\alpha\exp\left(\frac{-(1-\alpha)^{2}d\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)\right)}{\sqrt{1+(1-\alpha)^{2}\epsilon^{2}}}(1+o(1))&\mbox{if $\alpha\in(0,1)$}\\
O\left(1/\sqrt{N}\right)&\mbox{if $\alpha=0$}.\end{cases}

Hence setting
\alpha\in(0,1)
not only improves the asymptotic SNR rate in
N
, but increasing
\alpha
within that range also improves the leading order constant. For the DREP gradient estimator, it is worth highlighting that
\phi_{k}\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
is
0
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
as opposed to the case
\alpha=0
, meaning that letting
\alpha\in(0,1)
in this estimator improves the SNR rate in
N
(note that the setting where
\psi\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=0
for certain values of
\alpha
seems unlikely enough in practice to warrant a specific theoretical study of the leading order term in the SNR).

Example 2

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\phi=(\tilde{a},\tilde{b})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

A=\mathrm{diag}(\tilde{a})
. Set

p_{\theta}(z)=\mathcal{N}(z;\theta,\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
,

p_{\theta}(x|z)=\mathcal{N}(x;z,\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
and

q_{\phi}(z|x)=\mathcal{N}(z;Ax+\tilde{b},2/3\penalty\ \boldsymbol{I}_{d})

as in
Rainforth et al., 2018
. Consider the reparameterization
given by
Z=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varepsilon+Ax+\tilde{b}
,
where
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
. Then, the
assumptions from
Theorems

2
,
3
and
4

are met and all the terms appearing in these results are
analytically tractable. In particular, denoting

x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
,
\theta=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d})
,

\tilde{b}=(\tilde{b}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{d})
, letting

Ax+\tilde{b}=(\theta+x)/2+\epsilon\boldsymbol{u}_{d}
with

\epsilon>0
, we have that: for all

\alpha\in[0,1)
and
k=1\ldots d
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=-\frac{6\epsilon\alpha}{4-\alpha}-\frac{{24\epsilon(1-\alpha)(4-\alpha)^{d-1}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{24(1-\alpha)^{2}d\epsilon^{2}}{(5-2\alpha)(4-\alpha)}}}{N3^{d/2}(5-2\alpha)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}}+o\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),\quad\mbox{as $N\to\infty$}

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{4(4-\alpha)^{d/2}}{(15-6\alpha)^{d/4}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{12(1-\alpha)^{2}}{(4-\alpha)(5-2\alpha)}d\epsilon^{2}}\sqrt{{\frac{2}{5-2\alpha}+\left(\frac{12(1-\alpha)\epsilon}{(5-2\alpha)(4-\alpha)}\right)^{2}}}

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{16}\penalty\ \tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}V^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x),\quad\alpha\in(0,1)

and
\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}V^{(0,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
is given by (
165
) in
Section

C.2.3
. The derivation details for this example are deferred to
Section

C.2.3
.

Example

2
illustrates again the bias-variance tradeoff occurring in the REP and DREP gradient estimators as
\alpha
varies. Furthermore, the reduction in asymptotic variance obtained by using the DREP gradient estimator over the REP one is remarkably simple to visualize when
\alpha\in(0,1)
: it is captured by a multiplicative factor
{\alpha^{2}}/{16}
, which itself translates into a direct improvement in terms of asymptotic SNR: as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]={4}{\alpha}^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{SNR}[\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)],\quad\mbox{$\alpha\in(0,1)$}.

3.4
Gradient estimators in deteriorating variational approximation regimes

The asymptotic results of
Section

3.3
are
obtained as
N\to\infty
for a given model and fixed parameters

\theta
and
\phi
. However, how large
N
needs to be in practice for the provided asymptotic behaviors to kick in depends on how big the mismatch between the target density and the variational density is.

One way to see this is to keep
\alpha\in[0,1)
fixed in

Examples

1
and
2
and to focus on the impact of
d
and

\epsilon
in those examples. Increasing
d
or
\epsilon
can be interpreted as worsening the mismatch between the posterior density
p_{\theta}(\cdot|x)
and its variational approximation
q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)
: as
d
or
\epsilon
increase, the main terms in the predicted asymptotic behaviors of
Examples

1
and
2
vanish, and the variances will blow up. Consequently, as the mismatch between the posterior and variational densities grows, the sample size
N
required to reach the asymptotic regimes predicted by
Theorems

2
,
3
and
4
may become prohibitively large. This calls for a specific study of importance-weighted VI gradient estimators in settings where the variational approximation of the posterior density deteriorates.

To that end, we consider in the following an asymptotic regime where
N\to\infty
in such a way that the mismatch between the target density and the variational one increases in the meantime. Since this mismatch can be the result of various causes, we start by picking a criterion reflecting this mismatch, before making and motivating assumptions that enable us to carry out the analysis. Specifically, we propose to use the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right):=\ell(\theta;x)-\mathrm{ELBO}(\theta,\phi;x)

(35)

as a natural measure of mismatch and we seek to capture the behavior of importance-weighted VI gradient estimators as
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
. Here, it is understood that the considered target and/or variational density depend on
N
in any possible way, such as through
\theta
,
\phi
, or
x
(for example via their dimensions) but with the constraint that both
N\to\infty
and
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
. This regime is highly challenging since the gap between the marginal log-likelihood and the ELBO (that is, the VR-IWAE bound with
N=1
) goes to infinity and the variational approximation thus severely deteriorates.

We now introduce two assumptions that enable a rigorous treatment of this regime for the REP gradient estimator (
14
). The first assumption is on the log-weight
\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)
:

(B3)

For
\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}
, the
random field
\left(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right)_{(\theta,\phi)\in\Theta\times\Phi}
is a Gaussian
random field.

The second assumption, denoted
(B

df

REP

)
, guarantees that we can interchange derivative and expectation signs as needed when
(B3)
also holds. It is postponed to
Section

A.2
to not overload the paper with technical assumptions that can be overlooked in a first read and we concentrate instead on
(B3)
. Assumption
(B3)
permits us to use the convenient representation

\displaystyle\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)=\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x))-\sqrt{\mathbb{V}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}\left(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right)}S(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi;x),

(36)

where
\left(S(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi;x)\right)_{(\theta,\phi)\in\Theta\times\Phi}
is a centered Gaussian random field with unit variance. Intuitively, as importance weights often asymptotically approach a log-normal distribution
(see, e.g.,
Daudel et al., 2023
, for an explanation via the Central Limit Theorem)
, this assumption is meant to capture the limiting behavior where the weights are exactly log-normal. Based on (
36
), we can also link our measure of mismatch to the variance of the log-weights as

\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{V}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}\left(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right)=\ell(\theta;x)-\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}\left(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right)=\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right),

(37)

which follows from
(A4)
, (
35
) and the formula for exponential moments of Gaussian variables

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}\left(\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}\left(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{V}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}\left(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right)}.

The representation (
36
) will turn out to be key in our proofs since the REP gradient estimator involves differentiating
\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)
. We now investigate the asymptotic behavior of the REP gradient estimator (
14
) under
(B3)
-
(B

df

REP

)
as
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
. This leads us to the next proposition.

Proposition 1

Assume
(B3)
and

(B

df

REP

)
. Let
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
with

\displaystyle\log N=o\left(\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\right)

(38)

\displaystyle\limsup\left|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)\right|<1,

(39)

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x):=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{orr}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}\left(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x),\partial_{\psi}\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)\right).

(40)

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=O\left(\sqrt{\log N}\,\left|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)\right|\right),

(41)

with

\displaystyle\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x):=\mathrm{SNR}\left[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)-\mathbb{E}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)\right].

(42)

The proof of
Proposition

1
is deferred to

Section

D.4.1
.
Proposition

1

provides an upper bound on
\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
as
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
under two
conditions: the first condition
(
38
) ties
N
to
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
in such a
way that
N
should not grow quicker than
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)}
, while the
second condition (
39
) is a mild condition
to avoid the degenerate case where the log likelihood and its
derivative tend to be colinear (by requiring these correlations to
stay away from
1
and
-1
).

Here,
\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
captures whether the REP gradient estimator (
14
) with
N>1
behaves significantly differently from the case
N=1
. More precisely, it evaluates how the difference
\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))-\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
compares to the stochastic error in the REP gradient estimator, that is, if the shift obtained by using
N>1
instead of
N=1
is large enough to dominate the noise of the REP gradient estimator. In the theorem below, we provide more precise results characterizing its asymptotic behavior.

Theorem 5

Assume
(B3)
and
(B

df

REP

)
. Let
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
with (
38
) and

\sqrt{\log N}\,\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)=O(1).

(43)

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\log N}\;|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)|\left(1+o(1)\right).

(44)

If moreover,

\sqrt{\log N}\,\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)=o(1),

(45)

then we have

\mathrm{SNR}\left[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right]=\mathrm{SNR}\left[\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right]\,\left(1+o(1)\right)+o(1)\;.

(46)

The proof of
Theorem

5
can be found in
Section

D.4.2
.
Theorem

5
shows that, as
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
with (
38
) and under the condition (
43
) (which ensures (
39
) holds), the upper bound given in (
41
) is tight. In addition, it captures the asymptotic behavior of
\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
under the stronger condition (
45
). We now further delve into the interpretation of
Theorem

5
.

Assumptions made in
Theorem

5
.

As previously mentioned, (
38
) assumes that we are in a regime where
N
does not grow faster than
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)}
. We next elucidate the conditions (
43
) and (
45
). By appealing to the equivalent formulation of
|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)|
proved in (
194
) of
Section

D.3
, we get that (
43
) and (
45
) are assumptions on the behavior of

\displaystyle\sqrt{\log N}\left|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)\right|=\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{2\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)}}\mathrm{SNR}\left[{\partial_{\psi}\log\left(\frac{q_{\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)|x)}{p_{\theta}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)|x)}\right)}\right]

Hence, (
43
) and (
45
) are conditions on
{\partial_{\psi}\log\left({q_{\phi}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x|x))}/{p_{\theta}(f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)|x)}\right)}
, which is an estimator of
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
. As a result, the assumptions made in
Theorem

5
not only tie
N
to
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
, but also to an estimator of
\partial_{\psi}\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
: they quantify how the mismatch between the target density and the variational one evolves as a function of
N
.

Interpreting (
44
) and (
46
).

The asymptotic result (
44
) demonstrates that the optimization trajectory does not change significantly by increasing
N
beyond
N=1
in the REP gradient estimator (
14
), since
\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
is bounded as
N
grows (see
Remark

8
in the appendix for a finer analysis). Furthermore, under the stronger condition (
45
),
\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
reverts to
\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
by (
46
).

Hence, once the variational approximation deteriorates beyond the threshold of (
38
),
Theorem

5
suggests that utilizing
N>1
in importance-weighted VI methods imposes a computational burden without improving over the
N=1
ELBO baseline. However, a key takeaway message from
Theorem

5
is also that importance-weighted VI methods continue to yield valid gradient estimators for learning the parameters of interest
(\theta,\phi)
even when the variational approximation deteriorates significantly. Remarkably, reverting to the
N=1
ELBO baseline in fact ensures that the optimization trajectory refines the variational approximation by minimizing
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
w.r.t.
\phi
. Consequently, if the variational family is sufficiently expressive,
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
is expected to decrease during training, thus lowering the exponential threshold
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)}
and allowing a practical sample budget
N>1
to become algorithmically viable again. We illustrate
Theorem

5
by revisiting
Example

1
.

Example 3

Consider the setting of

Example

1
where
\theta-\phi=\epsilon\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{d}
with
\epsilon>0
. Then,
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}d
,
\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)=1/\sqrt{d}
and,
(
38
) and
(
45
) respectively become

\displaystyle\log N\ll\epsilon^{2}d\quad\text{and}\quad\log N\ll d\;.

(47)

Now applying
Theorem

5
: as
N,\epsilon^{2}d\to\infty
, we have under (
47
) that for all
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
\psi\in\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{d}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\epsilon\;(1+o(1))+o(1).

(48)

We also have that
\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=0
. The derivation details for this example are deferred to

Section

D.6
.

As
N,\epsilon^{2}d\to\infty
,
Example

3
states that
the condition (
47
) amounts to assuming that the number of
samples
N
does not grow quicker than exponentially with

\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon^{2}d}
, in which case the REP gradient estimator with

N>1
reverts to the REP gradient estimator with
N=1

(ELBO). Bypassing (
47
) imposes a heavy computational
budget as the latent space dimension
d
increases unless
\epsilon

decreases to compensate, that is, the variational approximation
closely matches the posterior density.

Note that
\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=0
for this example. As it
turns out, this property is also true if we consider a general
Gaussian model with known positive definite covariance matrix (see

Section

D.5
for details). Such models represent the
main models of interest satisfying
(B3)

and we argue that, although it is possible to study the DREP gradient
estimator as
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
for these models, it is better to skip
this study for the sake of conciseness in order to avoid lenghtly
derivations that are only applied to the corner case where

\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=0
. Instead, we wrap up this subsection by putting our results into perspective with the existing literature and by discussing extensions of the assumption
(B3)
.

Related work and broader impact.

The degradation of importance weights in high-mismatch regimes is a known phenomenon in the existing literature
(
Bengtsson et al., 2008
)
. Notably,
Agapiou et al., 2017
;
Chatterjee & Diaconis, 2018
analyze the sample size
N
leading to accurate estimation of self-normalized importance sampling (SNIS) estimators for bounded test functions.
Agapiou et al., 2017
obtain bounds on the bias and mean squared error of SNIS estimators suggesting
N
should be exponential with the KL divergence between the proposal and target distributions. Provided that the log-weight is concentrated around its expected value,
Chatterjee & Diaconis, 2018
demonstrate that having
N
of this order is necessary and sufficient to control the L1 error of SNIS estimators.

While
Agapiou et al., 2017
;
Chatterjee & Diaconis, 2018
establish the threshold below which SNIS estimators fail, these works provide no information on how this failure impacts the optimization trajectory of importance-weighted VI algorithms. Our work shows that the REP gradient estimator (
14
) does not break down when
N=o(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)})
but rather reverts to the ELBO case
N=1
and in particular minimizes
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
w.r.t.
\phi
. Hence,
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
is expected to decrease during training, thus reducing the sample budget
N>1
needed to escape the weight collapse regime. In addition, our results are obtained without assuming that
\alpha=0
(SNIS setting) and that the test function (in our case
\partial_{\psi}\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)
) is bounded. In doing so, we fill a known theoretical gap in the VI literature
(
Geffner & Domke, 2020
;
Vaitl et al., 2022
)
.

Relatedly,
Daudel et al., 2023
shows that the VR-IWAE bound reverts to the ELBO in the exact Gaussian random field case as well as in an instance of approximately Gaussian random field case. Our work departs from
Daudel et al., 2023
in two fundamental ways as (i) it shifts the focus from variational bounds to gradient estimators (ii) it considers the setting in which the variational approximation deteriorates and its deterioration manifests through the KL divergence
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
. In particular, moving from variational bounds to their gradient estimators has strong consequences in terms of proof techniques since we need to differentiate (
36
) in the proofs of
Proposition

1
and
Theorem

5
. To achive this, we rely on a novel bound which is key to show the collapse of self-normalized weighted averages (see
Proposition

7
in
Section

D.2
). Due to the generality of
Proposition

7
, we anticipate that this bound will be of use beyond our current work.

Beyond the assumption
(B3)
.

A logical extension of
(B3)
is to assume that the first two moments of
\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)
exist. We can then write without any loss of generality that

\displaystyle\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)=\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x))-\sqrt{\mathbb{V}_{\varepsilon\sim\tilde{q}}(\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x))}\tilde{S}(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime};x),

(49)

where
(\tilde{S}(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime};x))_{(\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime})\in\Theta\times\Phi^{2}}
is a centered and normalized random field. Under adequate conditions, the centered and normalized log likelihood ratio

\tilde{S}(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime};x)
is expected to be well approximated by a Gaussian random field. We conjecture that the gradient estimators
\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
and
\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
will revert to the case
N=1
under conditions akin to (and possibly stronger than) the ones from
Theorem

5
.

Adapting our results to this more general case in order to capture the behavior of both the REP and DREP gradient estimators requires further intricate derivations, which are left for future work. Indeed, our proofs build on the property that
S(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi;x)
and its derivative
\partial_{\psi}S(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi;x)
are two independent and centered Gaussian processes under
(B3)
(see
(

P
REP

)
in
Section

D.3
); the property
(

P
REP

)
does not hold anymore when we move away from
(B3)
to consider settings of the form (
49
) where
\tilde{S}(\varepsilon,\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime};x)
is well approximated by a Gaussian random field under adequate conditions.

3.5
Discussion and practical guidance

Our approaches in
Sections

3.3
and
3.4
are complementary approaches that characterize different behaviors/phases occuring importance-weighted VI algorithms.

On the one hand,
Section

3.3
captures the setting where the variational approximation matches the posterior density reasonably well, in which case the benefits of importance-weighted VI methods are fully leveraged. For this setting, increasing
N
leads to a variance reduction phenomenon and SNRs that scale with
\sqrt{N}
(except for edge cases), with the tuning of
\alpha
leading to further variance reduction. Furthermore, the gradient estimators
\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
and
\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
point on average in the direction that is expected to improve on ELBO maximization.

On the other hand,
Section

3.4
sheds light on the behavior of importance-weighted VI algorithms in the challenging regime where the
variational density is not suitably close to the posterior density in terms of KL divergence. While a weight collapse occurs when
N=o(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)})
where
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
can be large, this regime actively seeks to reduce
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
by improving the quality of the variational approximation.

These behaviors align with the two training phases hypothesized by
Vaitl et al., 2022
:
Section

3.4
describes the initial training phrase of importance-weighted VI algorithms and
Section

3.3
corresponds to the final training phase near convergence. While
Vaitl et al., 2022
treats these phases as decoupled, our analysis ties them together. Provided that the variational family is sufficiently expressive, the initial training phase indeed naturally drives the optimization trajectory toward the final one by lowering the exponential threshold
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)}
. From there, we can derive the following practical considerations:

Budget allocation.

Given a fixed total computational budget of
MN
(where
M
is the number of gradient steps), our results lead to a two-phase allocation strategy:

•

Initial training phase (high mismatch): Setting
N>1
imposes a strict computational penalty without altering the optimization trajectory. Therefore, the practitioner should set
N=1
to maximize
M
, allowing the model to traverse the parameter space as rapidly as possible to reduce
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
.

•

Final training phrase (low mismatch): When the variational approximation is sufficiently close to the target in terms of KL divergence, the practitioner should increase the budget to
N>1
.

On-the-fly tuning of
N
and
\alpha
.

Although
\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)
cannot be calculated on-the-fly, practitioners can cheaply monitor the Effective Sample Size (
\mathrm{ESS}
) or the variance of the log-weights as a proxy. For instance, if the monitored
\mathrm{ESS}
collapses toward
1
, it signals that the gradient estimators are suffering from a weight collapse. In this case, the practitioner can either automatically drop
N
to
1
to save computational effort, or dynamically tune
\alpha
toward a value that balances the bias-variance tradeoff we identified in this paper. A detailed investigation of these tuning mechanisms is left for future work.

High dimensions.

Although high dimensionality typically initializes the variational approximation into the initial training phrase, the fundamental quantity in importance-weighted VI is the mismatch between the variational approximation and the posterior density, rather than the latent dimension
d
itself. Our analysis suggests that, should the variational family be flexible enough, the variational approximation is improved during the optimisation procedure until it enters the first regime even in high dimensions. As we shall see now in our numerical experiments, one can perfectly emulate this high-dimensional behavior even within a moderate latent dimension
d
, so long as the variational approximation is sufficiently poor.

4
Numerical experiments

In this section, we provide empirical evidence supporting our theoretical claims.

4.1
Gaussian example

We consider the Gaussian example from
Example

1
, for which
Theorem

3
predicts the following asymptotic behavior of
\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
for all
\alpha\in[0,1)
: as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\dfrac{\sqrt{N}\epsilon\left(\alpha\exp\left(\frac{-(1-\alpha)^{2}d\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)+\frac{1-\alpha}{N}\exp\left(\frac{(1-\alpha)^{2}d\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)\right)}{\sqrt{1+(1-\alpha)^{2}\epsilon^{2}}}(1+o(1)).

To check the validity of this asymptotic result, we first study the case
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Consequently, we investigate whether the asymptotic behavior as
N\to\infty

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\dfrac{\sqrt{N}\epsilon\alpha\exp\left(\frac{-(1-\alpha)^{2}d\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{1+(1-\alpha)^{2}\epsilon^{2}}}(1+o(1)),\quad\mbox{$\alpha\in(0,1)$}

(50)

matches with the observed
\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
as
N
increases (note that only the leading order term remains in (
50
)). To this end, we let
\epsilon\in\{0.2,1.,2\}
,
\alpha\in\{0.1,0.3,0.5,0.7,0.9\}
,
d\in\{10,100\}
,
N\in\{2^{j},j=1\ldots 14\}
and
\phi_{k}
be a random coordinate in
(\phi_{1}\ldots\phi_{d})
. The results are shown in Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
Plotted is
\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
computed over 2000 Monte Carlo samples for the Gaussian example described
in
Section

4.1
as a function of
N
, for varying values of
(\alpha,d,\epsilon)
and a random coordinate
\phi_{k}
. The solid lines correspond to
\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]
, the dashed lines correspond to predictions of the form (
50
).

Observe first that, in the favourable setting of a low dimension
d
with a small perturbation
\epsilon
near the optimum (that is
(d,\epsilon)=(10,0.2)
), the asymptotic behavior predicted by (
50
) for each
\alpha
ends up matching the corresponding observed SNR as
N
increases. In particular, the bias-variance tradeoff behavior highlighted in our theoretical analysis of
Example

1
is empirically confirmed for
N
