), but starting from (
203
) instead of (
202
).

∎

We can now prove
Proposition

1
and
Theorem

5
.

D.4.1
Proof of
Proposition

1

The proof is made of two steps. Firstly we show that: as
N,B(\theta,\phi;x)\to\infty
with (
38
) holding,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\psi}\mathbb{E}(\Lambda_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon;x))+\sqrt{2\log N}\;\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\;\left(1+o(1)\right),

(208)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)^{2}\left(1+o(1)\right).

(209)

Secondly, we deduce the claim of the proposition.

(i)

Proof of (
208
) and (
209
).
Recall that

\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\dots
are i.i.d. copies of

\varepsilon
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{1,N},\ldots,\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{N,N}
as defined in (
185
) can be expressed as functions of
\Lambda_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{k};x)
for all
k=1\dots N
, by Property
(

P
REP

)
they are independent of the centered random variables
\partial_{\psi}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{1};x),\ldots,\partial_{\psi}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{N};x)
. Furthermore, Property
(

P
REP

)
also gives us that
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{1};x),\ldots,\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{N};x)
are i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Next noticing that
\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{1,N},\ldots,\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{N,N}
can be rewritten using
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{k};x)
for all
k=1\dots N
, we can apply
Lemma

8
with
\xi_{k}=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{k};x)
, and

\beta=(1-\alpha)B(\theta,\phi;x)
to prove (
208
) and (
214
). More precisely, by taking the expectation in (
188
) we get that

\mathbb{E}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)=\partial_{\psi}\mathbb{E}(\Lambda_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon;x))+\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\;\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\;\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{j};x)\right)

and, as
N,B(\theta,\phi;x)\to\infty
with (
38
)
holding, (
199
) with
\delta=1
gives us that (
208
) holds. Similarly, by taking the variance in (
188
) we have that

\mathbb{V}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)=\left(\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\right)^{2}\,\mathbb{V}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\;\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{j};x)\right)\\
+\left(B(\theta,\phi;x)\right)^{2}\,\mathbb{V}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\;\partial_{\psi}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{j};x)\right).

(210)

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)\geq\left(B(\theta,\phi;x)\right)^{2}\,\mathbb{V}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\;\partial_{\psi}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{j};x)\right)

and to get (
209
) it only remains to
show that the second variance in the right-hand side above is
(B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))^{2}\left(1+o(1)\right)
. Using Property
(

P
REP

)
again,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\;\partial_{\psi}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{j};x)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\;\partial_{\psi}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{j};x)\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\right)^{2}\;\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\partial_{\psi}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\theta,\phi}(\varepsilon_{j};x)\right)^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{(\alpha)}_{j,N}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=(B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))^{2}\left(1+o(1)\right)

(211)

where in the last line we have used (
201
) with
\delta=2
.

(ii)

We can now prove the claim of the proposition. By
Property
(

P
REP

)
, the stochastic terms in the
second line of (
191
) have mean zero and
by (
42
)
and (
208
), we have

\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{\sqrt{2\log N}\;\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\;\left(1+o(1)\right)}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)}}\;.

(212)

Applying (
209
), we get that

\mathrm{sSNR}_{\psi,{N}}^{({\alpha},\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\log N}\;\left|\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\right|\;\left(1+o(1)\right)}{B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\left(1+o(1)\right)}\;.

Now, by (
190
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\right|}{B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)}=\left(\left(\left|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)\right|\right)^{-2}-1\right)^{-1/2}

(213)

which is
O\left(\left|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)\right|\right)
under (
39
). Hence we get (
41
).

D.4.2
Proof of
Theorem

5

Since (
39
) is implied by (
43
), we can build on the equations established in the proof of
Proposition

1
such as (
210
). We then have that the first variance in the right-hand side of (
210
) is
o\left(\log N\right)
as

N,B(\theta,\phi;x)\to\infty

with (
38
) holding thanks to (
200
) with
\lambda=1
in

Lemma

8
. Pairing this up with (
211
) leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\right)^{2}\;o\left(\log N\right)

(214)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)^{2}\left(1+o(1)\right).

In addition, the condition (
43
) paired up with (
213
) yields

\frac{\left|\partial_{\psi}B(\theta,\phi;x)\right|}{B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)}=|\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)|\;\left(1+o(1)\right)=O\left(\left(\log N\right)^{-1/2}\right)\;.

so that (
214
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(B(\theta,\phi;x)\widetilde{B}^{(\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)^{2}\left(1+o(1)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))\left(1+o(1)\right)

(215)

Combining this with (
192
) and (
212
) leads to (
44
). Furthermore, (
189
), (
190
) and (
208
) yield

\mathbb{E}\left(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)\right)=\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))\\
+\sqrt{\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))}\sqrt{2\log N}\;\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)\left(1+o(1)\right).

(216)

If we further assume the stronger condition (
45
), (
46
) immediately follows from the two above displays paired up with (
192
) and (
208
).

Remark 8

As
N,\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)\to\infty
under (
38
) and (
43
), no improvement in variance is obtained by increasing the sample size
N
beyond the case
N=1
in
\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
since (
215
) shows that
\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
reverts to
\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
. The case of (
216
) is more subtle: using (
195
) in
Section

D.3
, (
216
) reads equivalently

\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))\\
+\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)}}\;\left[\partial_{\psi}\ell(\theta;x)-\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))\right]\left(1+o(1)\right).

(217)

Then,
\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
points in the direction of

\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
on average when
\psi\in\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{b}\}
, since
\partial_{\psi}\ell(\theta;x)=0
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)}}+o(1)\right).

There is no thus improvement in terms of gradient direction for learning
\phi
that follows from taking
N>1
instead of
N=1
. When
\psi\in\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{a}\}
, the answer depends on which term dominates in (
217
). Indeed,
\psi\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)
points on average (i) in the direction of
\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
should the first one dominate and (ii) in the direction of a convex combination of
\mathbb{E}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
and
\partial_{\psi}\ell(\theta;\phi)
should both terms be of the same order. Note that the case where the second term dominates amounts to assuming that
\mathrm{SNR}[\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=o(1)
under (
43
) so we leave it out.

D.5
General Gaussian model

Consider a general Gaussian model with known positive definite covariance matrix
\Sigma(x)
obtained by setting
p_{\theta}(z|x)=\mathcal{N}(z;\mu(\theta;x),\Sigma(x))

q_{\phi}(z|x)=\mathcal{N}(z;\tilde{\mu}(\phi;x),\Sigma(x))
, where
\mu
and
\tilde{\mu}
are the mean vectors parameterized by the true and variational parameters respectively and where we use the reparametrization
f(\varepsilon,\phi;x)={\Sigma(x)}^{1/2}\,\varepsilon+\tilde{\mu}(\phi;x)
with
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d})
. Then,
(B3)
holds and we have

\displaystyle\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)

\displaystyle=\ell(\theta;x)+\frac{1}{2}\langle\left(\tilde{\mu}(\phi;x)-\tilde{\mu}(\phi^{\prime};x)\right)\,,\,\Sigma(x)^{-1/2}\left(\tilde{\mu}(\phi;x)-\tilde{\mu}(\phi^{\prime};x)\right)\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\langle\left(\tilde{\mu}(\phi^{\prime};x)-\mu(\theta;x)\right)\,,\,\Sigma(x)^{-1}\left(\tilde{\mu}(\phi^{\prime};x)-\mu(\theta;x)\right)\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\langle\varepsilon\,,\,\Sigma(x)^{-1/2}\left(\tilde{\mu}(\phi;x)-{\mu}(\theta;x)\right)\rangle.

Observe then that the stochastic part above does not depend on
\phi^{\prime}
hence
\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x))

=0
when
\psi\in\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\}
. We also obtain that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\Delta(\theta,\phi;x)\right\|^{2}\;\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{\langle\partial_{\psi}\Delta(\theta,\phi;x)\,,\,\Delta(\theta,\phi;x)\rangle}{\left\|\Delta(\theta,\phi;x)\right\|\,\left\|\partial_{\psi}\Delta(\theta,\phi;x)\right\|}

with
\Delta(\theta,\phi;x):=\Sigma(x)^{-1/2}\left(\tilde{\mu}(\phi;x)-\mu(\theta;x)\right)
.

D.6
Proof of

Example

3

This
example is a specific case of the general one in

Section

D.5
with
\Sigma(x)=\mathbf{I}_{d}
,

\mu(\theta;x)=\theta
and
\tilde{\mu}(\phi;x)=\phi
. Thus we get
from
Section

D.5
that
(B3)

holds,
\Delta(\theta,\phi;x)=\phi-\theta
, wich implies

\|\partial_{\psi}(\phi-\theta)\|=1
and

\langle\partial_{\psi}(\phi-\theta)\,,\,(\phi-\theta)\rangle=\psi
for
any component
\psi
among

\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{a},\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{b}\}
, and:

\displaystyle\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)=\ell(\theta;x)-\frac{\|\theta-\phi\|^{2}}{2}-\langle\varepsilon+\phi^{\prime}-\phi,\phi-\theta\rangle\;,

(218)

\displaystyle\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi}(\varepsilon;x)=\ell(\theta;x)-\frac{\|\theta-\phi\|^{2}}{2}-\langle\varepsilon,\phi-\theta\rangle\;,

(219)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\|\theta-\phi\|^{2}\;,

(220)

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)=\frac{\psi}{\|\phi-\theta\|}\;.

(221)

We easily get from (
219
) that

(B

df

REP

)
also holds. Furthermore in the setting
\theta-\phi=\epsilon\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{d}
, we get that

\mathrm{KL}\left(\theta,\phi;x\right)=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}d\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{C}_{\psi}(\theta,\phi;x)=d^{-1/2}\;.

Therefore, in this specific case, (
38
) is equivalent
to (
47
), (
39
) to eventually have

d\geq 2
and (
43
)
and (
45
) to
\log N=O(d)

and
\log N\ll d
, respectively.

Applying
Theorem

5
, (
47
) then implies (
46
)
which takes the form (
48
) since (
219
) straightforwardly leads to

\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\epsilon\quad\text{for $k=1,\dots,a$.}

Furthermore, as far as the DREP estimator is concerned,
we get from (
218
) that

\displaystyle\left.\left[\partial_{\phi^{\prime}_{k}}\log\tilde{w}_{\theta,\phi,\phi^{\prime}}(\varepsilon;x)\right]\right|_{\phi^{\prime}=\phi}-0=0+\phi_{k}-\theta_{k}\;,

thus
\mathbb{V}(\psi\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x))=0
.

Appendix E
Additional numerical experiments

E.1
Gaussian experiment from
Section

4.1

Example

3
predicts that the REP gradient estimator with
N>1
reverts to the case
N=1
as
d
increases, and notably that
\mathbb{E}(\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
reverts to
\mathbb{E}(\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,1}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
. Similarly to the REP case detailed in
Section

4.1
, this behavior can be observed in the DREP case by setting a high value for
\epsilon
, see Figure
5
.

Figure 5:
Plotted is
\mathbb{E}(\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
computed over 2000 Monte Carlo samples for the Gaussian example described
in
Section

4.1
as a function of
N
, for varying values of
(\alpha,d)
,
\epsilon=2
and a random coordinate
\phi_{k}
. The solid lines correspond to
\mathbb{E}(\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x))
, the dashed lines correspond to predictions of the form
y=\epsilon\alpha
.

The IWAE case unfolds in the same manner as
d
increases. By
Example

1
: as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(0,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}\dfrac{\exp\left(\frac{d\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{1+\epsilon^{2}}}(1+o(1))

(222)

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\phi_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(0,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\frac{\sqrt{N}(1+o(1))}{\left({\exp\left(4d\epsilon^{2}\right)-4\exp\left(2d\epsilon^{2}\right)+4\exp\left(d\epsilon^{2}\right)-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}

(223)

We let
d\in\{10,100\}
,
\epsilon=0.2
,
N\in\{2^{j},j=1\ldots 15\}
and our results are plotted on Figure
6
. Similarly to the REP case with
\alpha\in(0,1)
detailed in
Section

4.1
, in the favourable setting of a low dimension
d=10
, the behavior of the REP
and DREP gradient estimators predicted by (
222
) and (
223
) respectively
match as
N
increases. As expected, this is no longer true as
d
increases.

and with

. The solid lines correspond to the SNRs, while the dashed lines correspond to predictions of the form (

) and (

).

E.2
Linear Gaussian experiment from
Section

4.2

We provide here additional experiments for the cases
\psi=\theta_{k}
with
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
\psi=\tilde{b}_{k}
with
\alpha=0
. By
Example

2
: as
N\to\infty
, for all
\alpha\in[0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\theta_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(\alpha,\mathrm{REP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\sqrt{N}\penalty\ \frac{\left|\frac{x_{k}-\theta_{k}}{2}+\frac{3\epsilon\alpha}{4-\alpha}\right|(1+o(1))}{\frac{(4-\alpha)^{d/2}}{(15-6\alpha)^{d/4}}\exp\left(\frac{12(1-\alpha)^{2}}{(4-\alpha)(5-2\alpha)}d\epsilon^{2}\right)\sqrt{{\frac{2}{5-2\alpha}+\left(\frac{12(1-\alpha)\epsilon}{(5-2\alpha)(4-\alpha)}\right)^{2}}}}

(224)

\displaystyle\mathrm{SNR}[\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}g_{1,N}^{(0,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)]=\sqrt{N}\frac{\frac{{24\epsilon 4^{d-1}}\exp\left(\frac{24d\epsilon^{2}}{4\cdot 5}\right)}{3^{d/2}5^{\frac{d}{2}+1}}}{\sqrt{\tilde{b}_{k}\text{-}V^{(0,\mathrm{DREP})}(\theta,\phi;x)}}(1+o(1))

(225)

We let
d\in\{10,100,500\}
,
\epsilon\in\{0.2,1\}
,
N\in\{2^{j},1\ldots 15\}
and our results are plotted on Figures
7
and
8
. Similarly to the cases detailed in
Section

4.1
, in the favourable setting of a low dimension
d=10
, the behavior of the REP
and DREP gradient estimators predicted by (
224
) and (
225
) respectively
match as
N
increases. This is no longer true as
d
increases. Interestingly, we see that when it comes to the learning of
\theta
, the SNR is not monotonic in
\alpha
, which is not surprising given the numerator term
\left|\frac{x_{k}-\theta_{k}}{2}+\frac{3\epsilon\alpha}{4-\alpha}\right|
appearing in (
224
).
